<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">

</div>

<div>PMCID: PMC12107345  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40433030/" class="usa-link">40433030</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>This study investigated the metabolic profiles and antibacterial activities of <em>Parkinsonia aculeata</em> leaves, stems, and fruits. Butanol extracts were analyzed using LC-ESI-MS/MS, tentatively identifying 116 secondary metabolites based on fragmentation patterns, biosynthetic pathways, and literature comparisons. Spectral similarity networks generated through the Global Natural Products Social Network (GNPS) revealed chemical similarities and identified 6 uncommon flavone compounds. Spectral similarity analysis revealed a close chemical resemblance between leaves and stems, while fruits exhibited distinct profiles. Antibacterial activity was assessed against seven pathogenic strains using both disk diffusion and microbroth dilution methods. Leaf extracts demonstrated the strongest activity, with inhibition zones up to 20.13 mm and MIC values as low as 1.5 mg mL<sup>−1</sup>, particularly against <em>S. aureus</em> ATCC 29213. Stem extracts showed comparable efficacy, while fruit extracts were more effective against <em>K. pneumoniae.</em> These findings highlight <em>P. aculeata</em> as a promising natural source of bioactive compounds for potential antimicrobial applications.</p></section><section class="abstract" id="abstract2"><hr class="headless">
<p>Metabolomic profiling and antibacterial assessment of different extracts from Egyptian <em>Parkinsonia aculeata</em> L.<a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12107345_d5ra00906e-ga.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/85d0/12107345/8298a756ac16/d5ra00906e-ga.jpg" loading="lazy" id="ga" height="378" width="747" alt="graphic file with name d5ra00906e-ga.jpg"></a></p></section><section id="sec1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p>Herbal medicines and their derivative products are widely used as therapeutic products in many countries. Their worldwide use has increased in the last decade. There are hundreds of active components in these herbs, and it is almost impossible to identify all these compounds.<sup><a href="#cit1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit1">1</a></sup> Various mass spectrometric techniques have been gradually employed for the investigation of medicinal plants to profile their secondary metabolites of plants. Liquid chromatography-electro-spray ionization tandem mass spectrometry (LC-ESI-MS/MS) is a widely recognized method for plant extract metabolite fingerprinting.<sup><a href="#cit2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit2">2–5</a></sup> Infectious diseases, primarily caused by bacteria, viruses, rickettsia, and fungi, continue to be significant. Although synthetic antibiotics have been extensively developed to manage these infections, their overuse has contributed to the emergence of antibiotic-resistant strains, creating a serious public health concern. Consequently, there is an urgent need to discover safe and effective antibacterial agents. Natural products derived from plants, particularly those rich in secondary metabolites like flavonoids and tannins, have shown great promise as safer alternatives. Medicinal plants have been traditionally used for generations, valued for their low cost, wide availability, and perceived safety.<sup><a href="#cit6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit6">6</a></sup></p>
<p>
<em>Parkinsonia aculeata</em> L., South American native that was, first observed in the wild in Egypt. It was noted during the vegetation survey of Elephantine Island in the Aswan Governorate, then in the Western Desert's Dakhla Oasis. A macromorphological comparison of the gathered specimens with specimens from other countries' herbariums and live specimens grown in Alexandria's Antoniades Botanic Garden verified the species identity.<sup><a href="#cit7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit7">7</a></sup><em>P. aculeata</em> L. is a species of the third-largest flowering plant family called Fabaceae. This family contributes important to human society. It has a lot of beneficial species, including woody, medicinal, and vegetable plants. <em>P. aculeata</em> is a big, prickly shrub or small tree, that can grow to a height of over 4 meters. This plant is cultivated for its beautiful and exquisite yellow flowers. It also possesses an amazing ability for survival and growth in a variety of soil types and environmental circumstances.<sup><a href="#cit8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit8">8</a></sup></p>
<p>According to the studies of the genus <em>Parkinsonia</em>, it contains variable classes of polyphenolic constituents as flavonoids; flavones/falvonols and their glycosides. In addition to terpenoids, saponins, fatty acids, tannins, sterols, alkaloids, and amino acids. <em>P. aculeata</em> L. has a number of documented health benefits, including antimalarial (leaves and flowers), smooth muscle stimulant, anti-inflammatory, hepatoprotective (leaves extract), antioxidant, hypoglycemic (aqueous extract of aerial part and bark), antispermatogenic (ethanolic extract of stem bark),<sup><a href="#cit9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit9">9,10</a></sup> antimicrobial, analgesic, and antipyretic.<sup><a href="#cit11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit11">11</a></sup> Pregnant women may use the boiling aqueous extract of its leaves as an abortifacient., therefore, <em>P. aculeata</em> may a herbal medicine.<sup><a href="#cit12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit12">12</a></sup></p>
<p>Under optimized conditions of pH, temperature, and concentration, the ethanol extract of <em>P. aculeata</em> has demonstrated promising antibacterial activity against various bacterial infections in biological systems.<sup><a href="#cit13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit13">13</a></sup> Moreover, both hexane and methanol extracts have shown significant antibacterial effects at defined concentrations. Similarly, crude leaf extracts obtained using ethanol, petroleum ether, and chloroform exhibited notable inhibitory activity against several bacteria species. The leaves and bark of <em>P. aculeata</em> exhibit significant therapeutic benefits for the treatment of various diseases, mainly due to their strong antimicrobial, antibacterial, and antioxidant activities. Polyphenols be responsible for the antimicrobial activity of dried aerial parts and dried stem bark.<sup><a href="#cit14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit14">14</a></sup></p>
<p>In our study we selected the butanol extract of leaves, stems, and fruits from Egyptian <em>P. aculeata</em> for antibacterial effects because evaluating its antibacterial potential provides novel insights and contributes valuable data to the scientific literature. Preliminary chromatographic analyses revealed that it is particularly rich in bioactive secondary metabolites, especially polyphenolic compounds, which are known for their strong antimicrobial properties.<sup><a href="#cit15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit15">15</a></sup> Moreover, the studies conducted on the Egyptian <em>P. aculeata</em> are rare, and did not consider the chemical profiles by LC-ESI-MS/MS of its butanol extract form different organs as well as their antimicrobial effect. This work represents the first comprehensive analysis of the bioactive compounds responsible for the antibacterial efficacy of the butanol extracts from the leaves, stems, and fruits of <em>P. aculeata</em>.</p>
<p>Based on the previous therapeutic benefits, antimicrobial effects of <em>P. aculeata</em> extracts, and its beneficial bioactive compounds, we maintaining our objective of studying antibacterial potential of butanol extract (BE) from leaves (L), stems (S), and fruits (F) in relation to their chemical profiling. Our current work aimed to (i) utilize LC-ESI-MS/MS analysis along with molecular networking to comprehensively characterize and map the chemical composition of the butanol extracts (BE) from the leaves, stems, and fruits of <em>P. aculeate</em> (ii) evaluate the antibacterial activity of the butanol extracts from different plant parts (leaves, fruits, and stems) against selected pathogenic bacteria; and (iii) identify the bioactive constituents responsible for the observed antibacterial effects (<a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig1"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 1. 
<em>P. aculeata</em> tree (A); leaves (B), fruits (C), and stems (D).</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12107345_d5ra00906e-f1.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/85d0/12107345/52092b17552a/d5ra00906e-f1.jpg" loading="lazy" height="167" width="708" alt="Fig. 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></figure></section><section id="sec2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Materials and methods</h2>
<section id="sec3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Plant-based components</h3>
<p>Aerial parts of <em>P. aculeata</em> (<a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1A</a>) were collected in May 2019, during the plant's flowering period, from the Orman Botanical Garden, Dokki, Giza, Egypt. The species was taxonomically identified by Dr M. El-Gibaly, Lecturer of Taxonomy and Consultant for the Central Administration of Plantation and Environment. A voucher specimen (No. T79) was deposited at the Chemistry of Tanning Materials and Leather Technology Department, National Research Centre, Dokki, Cairo, Egypt. As the plant was cultivated as an ornamental tree within the botanical garden, no special permit was required for its collection. The authors confirm that no protected or endangered species were involved in this study. After collection, the plant samples were thoroughly cleaned to remove dust, air-dried in the shade for 15 days, and then ground into a fine powder using a grinder (IKA® MF 10 Basic Microfine Grinder Drive, Breisgau, Germany). The powdered samples were stored in paper bags in the refrigerator until further analysis.</p></section><section id="sec4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Chemicals and drugs</h3>
<p>Di-ionized water (DI-H<sub>2</sub>O) is further purified using the Milli-Q Plus water purification equipment from Millipore Ltd in Bedford, Massachusetts, as well as analytical-grade (CH<sub>3</sub>OH, acetonitrile; CAN, formic acid) and HPLC DMSO from Merck in Darmstadt, Germany. The following substances were bought from Sigma Chemical Co.</p></section><section id="sec5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Extraction method and fractionation</h3>
<p>Air-dried and powdered leaves (L), stems (S), and fruits (F) of <em>P. aculeata</em> (1.5 kg each) were separately extracted with 70% aqueous methanol (CH<sub>3</sub>OH : H<sub>2</sub>O) under reflux at 60 °C. Each plant part was extracted in five successive batches (5 × 6 L) until complete exhaustion, as monitored by thin-layer chromatography (TLC). The combined extracts for each part were filtered and concentrated under reduced pressure at 50 °C using a rotary evaporator, yielding crude methanolic residues: approximately 120 g from the leaves, 50 g from the stems, and 100 g from the fruits. Each dried residue was suspended in a mixture of methanol and water (CH<sub>3</sub>OH : H<sub>2</sub>O, 1 : 9, v/v; 300 mL) and subjected to successive liquid–liquid partitioning with solvents of increasing polarity: petroleum ether (60 : 80), chloroform (CHCl<sub>3</sub>), ethyl acetate (EtOAc), and <em>n</em>-butanol (<em>n</em>-BuOH) (<a href="#sch1" class="usa-link">Scheme 1</a>). After solvent partitioning, the obtained fractions were as follows: petroleum ether fraction: 5 g (L), 0.5 g (S), and 2.5 g (F); chloroform fraction: 7 g (L), 2 g (S), and 5 g (F); ethyl acetate fraction: 16 g (L), 5.5 g (S), and 9.5 g (F); <em>n</em>-butanol fraction: 28 g (L), 8 g (S), and 15 g (F).<sup><a href="#cit9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit9">9</a></sup></p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="sch1"><h4 class="obj_head">Scheme 1. Extraction and fractionation of <em>P. aculeata</em> leaves (L), stems (S), and fruits (F).</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12107345_d5ra00906e-s1.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/85d0/12107345/d0649c996fe7/d5ra00906e-s1.jpg" loading="lazy" height="567" width="779" alt="Scheme 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/sch1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></figure><p>Preliminary chromatographic profiling (TLC and HPLC) revealed that the ethyl acetate and <em>n</em>-butanol fractions were rich in polyphenolic compounds, whereas the petroleum ether and chloroform fractions showed a low content of these metabolites. Based on these findings, the <em>n</em>-butanol fractions were selected for further phytochemical investigation by LC-ESI-MS/MS analysis.</p></section><section id="sec6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. Sample preparation for HPLC profiling and MS analysis</h3>
<p>The lyophilized BuOH extracts of the three organs (50 mg each) were adequately dissolved in 70% CH<sub>3</sub>OH (HPLC-grade) with sonication (10 min), and particulate matter was removed, allowing for a clearer sample for analysis, then centrifuged. After evaporating the aliquots under reduced pressure, they were freeze-dried for 48 hours. About 10 mg of the dried BuOH extracts were dissolved in 500 mL of CH<sub>3</sub>OH (HPLC-grade), each in triplicates, and 10 mL were injected for HPLC profiling. For the MS/MS analysis, three copies of 10 mg in 250 mL of CH<sub>3</sub>OH (MS-grade) were prepared using 5 mL as the injection volume in the LC-MS/MS analysis.<sup><a href="#cit5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit5">5</a></sup></p></section><section id="sec7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.5. Equipment and conditions</h3>
<section id="sec8"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.5.1. HPLC/ESI-MS/MS analysis</h4>
<p>The sample was analyzed using liquid chromatography-electrospray ionization tandem mass spectrometry (LC-ESI-MS/MS) with a SCIEX Triple Quad 5500+ MS/MS system fitted with an electrospray ionization (ESI) detector for detection and an Exion LC AC system for separation. Ascentis® C18 Column (4.6 × 150 mm, 3 μm) was used for the separation. The mobile phases included two eluents: acetonitrile (HPLC-grade) and 0.1% formic acid (A). The gradient of mobile phase was set up as follows: 0% B at 0–2 minutes, 10% at 36.1, 10% from 36.1–40 minutes, 90% at 2–30 minutes, and 90% at 30–36 minutes. The flow rate was 0.7 mL min<sup>−1</sup>, while 10 μL was the injection volume. With a scan (EMS-IDA-EPI) from 100 to 1000 Da for MS1, negative ionization mode (−ve) was used for MS/MS analysis with the following parameters: ion spray voltage: −4500; source temperature: 500 °C; curtain gas: 25 pressure; ion source gas 1 and 2 were 45 psi and ranged from 50 to 1000 Da for MS2, with a collision energy of −35 and a collision energy spread of −80. The MS-DIAL software version 4.70 and the Fiehn HILIC library were used to identify the constituents.</p></section></section><section id="sec9"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.6. Molecular networking</h3>
<p>The raw data files generated from LC-ESI-MS/MS analysis were converted into mzXML format using the MS Convert tool from ProteoWizard (version 3.0.21050, <a href="https://www.proteowizard.org" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://www.proteowizard.org</a>). Once converted, the data were uploaded to the MassIVE Data sets repository <em>via</em> WinSCP (<a href="https://www.massive.ucsd.edu" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://www.massive.ucsd.edu</a>). Molecular networking (MN) of the LC-ESI-MS/MS data was performed using the Global Natural Product Social (GNPS) platform. The analysis parameters included a mass tolerance of 0.2 Da for precursor ions and 0.05 Da for fragment ions, adhering to the default GNPS settings. A minimum of six MS fragments was required to establish matches between consensus MS/MS spectra, with a cosine score threshold of 0.7 set to define similarity between nodes. The resulting molecular network was exported as a GraphML file and visualized using a force-directed layout in Cytoscape version 3.7.1, following methods detailed in prior studies.<sup><a href="#cit16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit16">16,17</a></sup></p></section><section id="sec10"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7. Antibacterial assay of <em>P. aculeata</em> extracts (leaves, stems, and fruits)</h3>
<section id="sec11"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7.1. Bacterial strains</h4>
<p>All bacterial strains, including the standard strains [<em>Staphylococcus aureus</em> ATCC 29213 (<em>S. aureus</em>), <em>Pseudomonas aeruginosa</em> ATCC 27853 (<em>P. aeruginosa</em>), <em>Escherichia coli</em> ATCC 25922 (<em>E. coli</em>)] and clinical isolates of <em>S. aureus</em>, <em>P. aeruginosa</em>, and <em>E. coli</em>, were sourced from the American Type Culture Collection (ATCC) in Rockville, MD, USA, and the Northern Utilization Research and Development Division of the United States Department of Agriculture (NRRL) in Peoria, IL, USA. Before conducting the bioassay, the bacterial strains were rejuvenated by sub-culturing them in a fresh nutrient broth medium for 24 hours. This assay was performed at the Regional Centre for Mycology and Biotechnology, Al-Azhar University, Cairo, Egypt.</p></section><section id="sec12"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7.2. Biological assessment</h4>
<section id="sec13"><h5 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7.2.1. Disk diffusion assay</h5>
<p>The antibacterial activity of <em>P. aculeata</em> extracts (leaves, stems, and fruits) was evaluated using the disk diffusion method.<sup><a href="#cit18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit18">18</a></sup> Bacterial strains were initially cultured on Mueller–Hinton agar, except for <em>Streptococcus</em>, which was grown on Mueller–Hinton agar enriched with 50 mL L<sup>−1</sup> blood, at 37 °C for 24 hours before being transferred to nutrient agar. Sterile filter disks (6 mm in diameter, Whatman no. 3) were placed on agar plates inoculated with the bacterial suspension. Each disk was treated with 40 μL of the extract dissolved in 60% ethanol at various concentrations (2.00 mg mL<sup>−1</sup>, 1.00 mg mL<sup>−1</sup>, 0.50 mg mL<sup>−1</sup>). The prepared Petri dishes were stored at 4 °C for 1 hour and then incubated at 37 °C for 24 hours. Standard disk of vancomycin (30 μg) was used as reference control. Antibacterial activity was determined by measuring the inhibition zones around the disks. All experiments were conducted in triplicate, and the results were presented as mean ± standard deviation (SD).</p></section><section id="sec14"><h5 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7.2.2. Determination of minimum inhibitory concentration (MIC)</h5>
<p>The Minimum Inhibitory Concentration (MIC) was assessed to evaluate the antibacterial effectiveness of the plant extracts using the broth microdilution method, in line with the Clinical and Laboratory Standards Institute (CLSI) guidelines. Two-fold serial dilutions of the extracts were prepared in sterile nutrient broth, covering a concentration range from 250 mg mL<sup>−1</sup> to 0.47 mg mL<sup>−1</sup>, and were added to a 96-well microtiter plate. To each well, 100 μL of a bacterial suspension, adjusted to the 0.5 McFarland standard, was inoculated. The plates were then incubated at 37 °C for 24 hours, according to the specific growth conditions required for each bacterium. After the incubation period, the plates were examined for any visible bacterial growth. The MIC was determined as the lowest concentration of the extract that resulted in no visible microbial growth on the plate's surface.<sup><a href="#cit19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit19">19</a></sup></p></section><section id="sec15"><h5 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7.2.3. Statistical analysis</h5>
<p>The inhibition zones were expressed as means ± standard deviation (SD). The data significance was assessed using analysis of variance (ANOVA), performed with the Microsoft Excel program. Differences in the data were considered significant at a 5% level.</p></section></section></section></section><section id="sec16"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Results and discussion</h2>
<section id="sec17"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. Analysis of qualitative data</h3>
<p>We employed HPLC-ESI-MS/MS to characterize the phytochemical profile of the leaves, stems, and fruits of <em>P. aculeata</em> butanol extracts. This efficient and precise method for screening <em>P. aculeata</em> was enabled by the use of HPLC and the efficient extraction of the targeted plant parts. A negative ionization mode experiment was conducted to capture the diverse ionization and fragmentation characteristics of the polyphenolic metabolites in the BuOH extract of leaves, stems, and fruits. All substances were extracted and analyzed by considering solvent adducts and [M − H]<sup>−</sup> ions. The matched compounds were then characterized using established information, proposed fragmentation pathways, and additional data from the literature. This meticulous identification process revealed 116 distinct compounds were identified tentatively in the butanol extracts for the first time. The molecular networking facilitated the identification of 6 secondary metabolites. Flavonoids emerging as the predominant class of polyphenolics in <em>P. aculeata</em>. A diverse array of flavonoid derivatives, totaling approximately 71 compounds (19 flavonols, 31 flavones, 4 flavanones, 3 flavan-3ol, and 3 dihydrochalcones) were tentatively identified alongside other phytochemicals, including 19 phenolic acids, 5 lignans, 5 triterbens, 13 fatty acids and 2 organic acids. In addition to 6 unusual glycosides of flavones on the bases of GNPS libraries. The superimposed base peak intensity (BPI) chromatograms for the three extracts revealed a high degree of similarity between the leaves and stems, while the fruits extract showed significant variations, notably within the retention time (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub>) range of 11–21 min in the (−ve) negative mode (<a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2A–C</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig2"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 2. Total ion current chromatogram (TIC), and (B): base peak intensity chromatograms of <em>P. aculeata</em> butanol extracts from (A): leaves, (B): stems and (C): fruits using HPLC-ESI-MS/MS in (−ve) ionization mode.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12107345_d5ra00906e-f2.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/85d0/12107345/df47daf71eeb/d5ra00906e-f2.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1102" width="742" alt="Fig. 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></figure></section><section id="sec18"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. The annotation of metabolites and molecular networking</h3>
<section id="sec19"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.1. Annotation of uncommon glycosylated flavones based on GNPS library</h4>
<p>The molecular family of flavonoids, obtained from the molecular network in negative mode, revealed six rare flavone metabolites within cluster A (<a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a>). These metabolites were annotated for the first time in <em>Parkinsonia</em> species and were generally distributed across the three investigated plant parts, with a higher abundance in the leaves. Most of them were chrysoeriol isomers (A1–A4) with deprotonated ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 736.92, 766.96, 751.00, and 910.94, respectively. Two apigenin isomers were appeared at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 706.92, and 880.58. Their identification was based on GNPS library matches and their characteristic fragmentation patterns (<a href="#tab2" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). Annotation was derived from the abundant fragments of deprotonated ions and their corresponding derivatives, primarily resulting from the loss of 145 Da (C<sub>6</sub>H<sub>9</sub>O<sub>4</sub>˙), corresponding to the cleavage of 3-hydroxy-3-methylpentanedioic acid. Additionally, neutral losses of [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup>, [M-H-120]<sup>−</sup>, [M-H-60]<sup>−</sup>, and [M-H-104]<sup>−</sup> were observed, indicating sequential cleavage of hexose, pentose, and rhamnose units. Additional fragmentation generates ions compatible with [Ag + 113], [Ag + 83] indicative for di-<em>C</em>-glycosyl flavone fragmentation (ESI, Fig. S110–S115<a href="#fn1" class="usa-link">†</a>). This pattern supports the existence of isomers of apigenin and chrysoeriol. Finally, based on this fragmentation behavior and GNPS library the compounds (A1–A6) were identified tentatively as chrysoeriol 6-<em>C</em>-pentoside 8-<em>C</em>-hexosyl-6′′′[(3-hydroxy-3-methylpentanedioic acid)] (A1), chrysoeriol 6,8-di-<em>C</em>-hexosyl-6′′′[(3-hydroxy-3-methylpentanedioic acid)] (A2), chrysoeriol 6-<em>C</em>-rhamnoside-8-<em>C</em>-hexosyl-6′′′[(3-hydroxy-3-methylpentanedioic acid)] (A3), chrysoeriol 6,8-di-<em>C</em>-hexosyl-6′′,6′′′-di[(3-hydroxy-3-methylpentanedioic acid)] (A4), apigenin 6-<em>C</em>-pentoside 8-<em>C</em>-hexosyl 6′′′[(3-hydroxy-3-methylpentanedioic acid)] (A5); and apigenin 6,8-di-<em>C</em>-hexosyl-6′′,6′′′-di[(3-hydroxy-3-methylpentanedioic acid)] (A6).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig4"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 4. Comprehensive molecular network constructed from MS/MS data in negative ionization mode for butanol extracts of <em>P. aculeata</em> leaves, stems, and fruits. Cluster A represents uncommon glycosylated flavones, while cluster B consists of flavones identified for the first time in the stems and fruits. *Note: substitution positions may vary.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12107345_d5ra00906e-f4.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/85d0/12107345/6343937ba24e/d5ra00906e-f4.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1102" width="733" alt="Fig. 4"></a></p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/tab2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section><section id="sec20"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.2. Determination and tentative identification of common phytochemical constituents</h4>
<section id="sec21"><h5 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.2.1. Phenolic acids</h5>
<p>A total of 20 metabolites derived from phenolic acids and their glycosides were identified in butanol fractions and categorized into two main groups: hydroxybenzoic acids and hydroxycinnamic acids. Eight peaks corresponded to hydroxycinnamic acids and their glycosides, including cinnamic acid (1), ferulic acid (8), caffeic acid (6), caffeic acid hexoside (7), <em>O</em>-caffeoylquinic acid (5), coumaric acid (18), coumaric acid hexuornide (14), and coumaric acid di-<em>O</em>-hexuornide (16). Eleven ion intensity were attributed to hydroxybenzoic acids and their glycosides, which included <em>p</em>-hydroxybenzoic acid (15), <em>p</em>-hydroxybenzoic acid-4-<em>O</em>-hexoside (20), protocatechueic acid (17), protocatechuicaldehyde (2), vanillic acid (13), vanillic acid-<em>O</em>-hexoside (12), vanillic acid-<em>O</em>-rutinoside (10), gallic acid (19), methyl gallate (22), methyl gallate-<em>O</em>-hexoside (9), and hydroxy-anthranilic acid (11). Additionally, two organic acids were eluted first and detected: citric acid (3) and quinic acid (4). One phenolic compound, identified as 4-vinylphenol, was identified.</p>
<p>The analysis revealed that hydroxybenzoic acids were more abundant than hydroxycinnamic acids across all investigated plant parts (L, S, F). Their detailed MS/MS data, including how they fragmented and their elution times, are presented in (<a href="#tab1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). All these phenolic acids showed strong peaks in mass spectrometry, either as negatively charged ions (M<sup>−</sup> or [M − H]<sup>−</sup>), representing their deprotonated molecules, or as smaller fragments (daughter ions). These fragments resulted from losing a carbon dioxide (CO<sub>2</sub>) molecule; 44 amu or a water molecule (H<sub>2</sub>O); 18 amu from the original ion (base ion). The consistent loss of 44 amu (carbon dioxide) is a key indicator used to identify phenolic acids.<sup><a href="#cit20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit20">20</a></sup></p>

</div></div></section><section id="sec22"><h6 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.2.1.1. Free phenolic acids</h6>
<p>Nine free phenolic acids (1, 6, 8, 18, 17, 19, 11, 15, and 13) were tentatively identified in the butanol extracts based on their mass-to-charge ratios (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em>), retention times (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub>), and characteristic fragmentation patterns consistent with previously reported data.<sup><a href="#cit21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit21">21</a></sup> These phenolic compounds included cinnamic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 146.90, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 0.363), which exhibited fragment ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 102.95 [M-H-COO]<sup>−</sup> and 129.21 [M-H-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup>. Caffeic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 179.08, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 1.160) was characterized by fragment ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 161.06 [M-H-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup> and 135.05 [M-H-COO]<sup>−</sup>. Ferulic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 192.99, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 1.555) displayed fragment ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 174.98 [M-H-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup>, 156.94 [M-H-2H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup>, and 149.04 [M-H-COO]<sup>−</sup>. Coumaric acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 163.01, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 2.983) was identified with fragment ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 144.92 [M-H-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup> and 119.03 [M-H-COO]<sup>−</sup>. Protocatechuic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 153.00, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 2.972) exhibited fragment ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 135.01 [M-H-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup>, 116.92 [M-H-2H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup>, and 109.01 [M-H-COO]<sup>−</sup>. Gallic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 168.88, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 3.064) was characterized by fragment ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 151.89, 150.92 [M-H-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup>, 132.94 [M-H-2H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup>, and 125.05 [M-H-COO]<sup>−</sup>. These phenolic acids were detected in all plant parts examined. Hydroxy-anthranilic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 154.06, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 1.872) was exclusively identified in the fruit butanol extract, with fragmentation patterns at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 135.96 [M-H-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup>, 117.95 [M-H-2H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup>, and 110.05 [M-H-COO]<sup>−</sup>. <em>p</em>-Hydroxybenzoic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 137.00, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 2.498) was detected solely in leaves extract, with fragment ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 118.99 [M-H-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup> and 93.00 [M-H-COO]<sup>−</sup>. Vanillic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 167.01, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 2.223) was found in both the stem and fruit extracts, exhibiting fragmentation patterns at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 149.03 [M-H-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup>, 123.02 [M-H-COO]<sup>−</sup>, and 105.02 [M-H-H<sub>2</sub>O–COO]<sup>−</sup>, respectively.<sup><a href="#cit22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit22">22,23</a></sup></p></section><section id="sec23"><h6 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.2.1.2. Phenolic acid glycosides</h6>
<p>The main method for identifying and characterizing phenolic acid glycosides was to examine their fragmentation patterns, particularly the elimination of the glycosyl moiety and the carboxylic acid group. The presence or lack of the characteristic product ions produced by the carboxyl group's cleavage gave important information about where the sugar-linked sites were located in the glycoside molecules. With the use of this method, these compounds could be effectively differentiated and their structures clarified, underscoring the critical role that COO-group removal plays in their mass spectrometric analysis.<sup><a href="#cit24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit24">24</a></sup></p>
<p>In MS/MS analysis, five phytoconstituents identified as phenolic acid hexosides exhibited distinct fragmentation patterns, characterized by glycosidic bond cleavage and the absence of a typical CO<sub>2</sub> loss fragment. This behavior suggests that the glycosyl moiety is esterified to the phenolic molecule at the 1-<em>O</em>-position rather than being linked through an ether bond. For instance, MS/MS spectral data from the fruit revealed a key fragment at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 167.05 [M-H-162(hex)]<sup>−</sup> attributed to vanillic acid-<em>O</em>-hexoside (12). Similarly, the secondary ion detected in both the stem and fruit at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 167.05 [M-H-308(rutinose)]<sup>−</sup> was identified as vanillic acid-<em>O</em>-rutinoside (10). An ion fragment at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 183.11 [M-H-162(hexose)]<sup>−</sup> was assigned to methyl gallate-<em>O</em>-hexoside was observed exclusively in the stem (9). Additionally, a daughter ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 163.07 [M-H-176(glucuronic acid)]<sup>−</sup>/[M-H-352(di hexuornide)]<sup>−</sup> confirmed the presence of mono and di coumaric acid hexuornide (14, and 16) in leaves and stems, respectively. The fragmentation pattern of peak 7, highlighted by the production of key daughter ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 323.17 [M-H-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup>, <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 297.19 [M-H-COO]<sup>−</sup>, <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 179.03 [M-H-162(hex)]<sup>−</sup>, and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 135.05 [M-H-162(hex)-COO]<sup>−</sup>, reveals a stepwise loss of COO, H<sub>2</sub>O, and the sugar moiety. These distinctive ions, confirm that compound 7 as caffeic acid-<em>O</em>-hexoside detected in the extract's leaves, stems and fruits. Additionally, the data strongly indicate that the hexose is linked to caffeic acid through an ether bond rather than an ester bond. Similarly <em>p</em>-hydroxybenzoic acid hexosides (15) was tentatively identified in leaves butanol extract through its characteristic fragmentation profile. Key daughter ions observed at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 255.05 [M-H-COO]<sup>−</sup>, <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 237.03 [M-H-COO-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup>, <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 137.02 [M-H-162(hex)]<sup>−</sup>, and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 119.15 [M-H-162(hex)-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup> suggest that the sugar moiety is linked to <em>p</em>-hydroxybenzoic acid through an ether bond rather than an ester bond.<sup><a href="#cit25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit25">25</a></sup></p>
<p>Peak 5, the most abundant phenolic compound was identified across all examined plant parts, with its highest concentration observed in the fruits. The compound presented a parent ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 353.09 with a retention time of 1.127 min. Its fragmentation pattern revealed characteristic daughter ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 191.06, representing the loss of a caffeoyl group (162), and at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 179.03, corresponding to the loss of a quinate group (174). These findings strongly suggest that the compound is <em>O</em>-caffeoylquinic acid.</p></section></section><section id="sec24"><h5 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.2.2. Flavonoids</h5>
<p>The main aim of this study was to apply negative ionization mass spectrometry (MS) to identify flavonoids with notable biological activity and compare them to data from existing reports. The chemical structures of flavonoid derivatives were determined by examining their fragmentation patterns. Flavonoid glycosides tend to produce [M − H]<sup>−</sup> ions rather than [M + H]<sup>+</sup> ions. The MS/MS spectra revealed distinct fragment ions resulting from the retro-Diels–Alder fragmentation process, along with the loss of glycosyl groups in both (−ve) negative and (+ve) positive ion modes. Notable losses of ions included hexose (glucose or galactose), rhamnose, hexuronic acid (glucuronic acid), pentose (arabinose or xylose), and neohesperidoside (glucose + rhamnose), comparable to mass losses of 162, 146, 176, 132, and 308 amu, respectively, which were easily and specially apparent in the case of <em>O</em>-glycosides of flavonoids. Additionally, flavonoids showed a loss of 28 amu (CO), 18 amu (H<sub>2</sub>O), and 15 amu (CH<sub>3</sub>) confirming the presence of phenolic hydroxyl and CH<sub>3</sub> groups, aiding in the identification of different flavonoid subgroups.<sup><a href="#cit26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit26">26</a></sup></p>
<p>In compounds where two or more sugar molecules are attached to the same aglycone carbon, the ions generated from the cleavage of glycosidic bonds between the sugar units are typically weak. While the aglycone and glycane components are commonly identified. Determining the precise structure of flavonoid glycosides remains challenging. This difficulty arises because the identity of the monosaccharides and their specific attachment sites cannot always be established using LC-MS alone. Ultimately, the structures of these compounds were confirmed through comparison with previously reported data in the literature.<sup><a href="#cit27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit27">27</a></sup></p>
<p>LC-MS/MS analysis of <em>C</em>-glycosides revealed a key product ions [M-H-90(C<sub>3</sub>H<sub>6</sub>O<sub>3</sub>)]<sup>−</sup> &amp; [M-H-120(C<sub>4</sub>H<sub>8</sub>O<sub>4</sub>)]<sup>−</sup> characteristic for hexose cleavage and [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup> &amp;[M-H-60]<sup>−</sup> characteristic for pentose cleavage, as well as the absence of the [M-H-hexose]<sup>−</sup> and [M-H-pentose]<sup>−</sup> ions for ether-linked glycosides. In contrast to <em>O</em>-glycosides, which are easily loss the sugar moiety, due to the ease with which oxygen (O) becomes ionized and the fact that the O–C bond is more susceptible to cleavage than the C–C bond under MS analysis conditions.<sup><a href="#cit28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit28">28,29</a></sup></p>
<p>The <em>C</em>-glycosylated flavonoids prefer breaking apart on the glycosidic moiety, and the resulting ions are aglycones with connected sugar moieties. We identify the nature of the aglycone, whether it is a monoglycoside or a diglycoside, through their characteristic ions, as it loses [aglycone + 41]; [aglycone + 71] in mono-<em>C</em> and [aglycone + 83]; [Ag + 113] in di-<em>C</em>-glycoside. The majority of <em>C</em>-glycosides have sugars attached at the C-6 and/or C-8 positions, so it was important to distinguish between 6-<em>C</em> and 8-<em>C</em> glycosylated flavones and determine the type of sugar substituted at the <em>C</em>-6 and <em>C</em>-8 positions.<sup><a href="#cit30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit30">30,31</a></sup></p>
<p>In 6-<em>C</em>-glycosylated flavonoids, there is a clear loss of H<sub>2</sub>O in the deprotonated ion compared to 8-<em>C</em>-glycosylated flavonoids. In 6-<em>C</em>-glycosylated flavonoids H<sub>2</sub>O can easily lose between the OH- group at the 2′′-position of the sugar and its counterpart at the 5- or 7-OH position of the aglycone. However, the mechanism of H<sub>2</sub>O loss in 8-<em>C</em>-glycosyl flavonoids is limited to the 7-OH group and is inhibited by hydrogen bonding between the 7-OH group of the aglycone and the sugar atom. The relative ion intensities of [M-H-90] in 6-<em>C</em>-isomers are higher than in 8-<em>C</em>-isomers (ESI, Fig. S108), which is another notable distinction between the two isomers. Two characteristics of 2′′-<em>O</em>-glycosyl-<em>C</em>-glycosyl derivatives are the presence of an abundant ion [M-H-132/-146/-162]<sup>−</sup>, preferential fragmentation that results in a relevant [M-H-18]<sup>−</sup> fragment; and the <em>O</em>-glycosylation at position 6′′ of the sugar moiety from <em>C</em>-glycosylation, which causes loss [M-H-162-120]<sup>−</sup> and [M-H-162-90]<sup>−</sup> from <em>C</em>-glycosylation.<sup><a href="#cit30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit30">30,32</a></sup></p>
<section id="sec25"><h6 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.2.2.1. Flavonols</h6>
<p>
<strong>
<em>Kaempferol-based derivatives</em>
</strong>: Several kaempferol derivatives, peaks (23–25, 33, 35, and 40) were detected in the butanol extract of leaves, stems, and fruits. The majority of these compounds were found to be glycosides with a single sugar moiety. Kaempferol was identified in both <em>C</em>-glycosylated and <em>O</em>-glycosylated forms, distinguished by their characteristic MSn fragmentation patterns. The <em>C</em>-glycosylated forms exhibit a carbon–carbon bond that is resistant to cleavage, with fragmentation primarily occurring at the sugar bonds. In contrast, <em>O</em>-glycosylated kaempferol readily loses its sugar moiety through neutral loss.</p>
<p>The leaves extract was the only source of one such <em>O</em>-glycosylated kaempferol, referred to as compound 23. This compound exhibited an [M − H]<sup>−</sup> ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 430.93, and its MS/MS spectrum revealed a fragment ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 285.12, indicating a neutral loss of 146 amu, corresponding to rhamnoside. Further fragmentation of the <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 285 ion produced characteristic kaempferol fragments at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 241.11 and 223.08. Based on this data, compound 23 (ESI, Fig. S35) was identified as kaempferol-<em>O</em>-rhamnoside.<sup><a href="#cit33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit33">33</a></sup> The <em>C</em>-glycosylated kaempferol derivatives were identified in both leaves and stems. Compounds 24 and 33 exhibited the same [M − H]<sup>−</sup> ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 446.90, while compound 25 showed a [M − H]<sup>−</sup> ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 448.93, corresponding to its dihydro form. The MS/MS analysis revealed fragmentation patterns characteristic of mono-<em>C</em>-glycosides, with fragment ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 357, 327, 359, and 329, resulting from neutral losses of 120 and 90 amu, respectively. These neutral losses indicate the presence of a hexose moiety. Given that the 6-<em>C</em>-hexoside elutes before the 8-<em>C</em>-hexoside isomer, compounds 24, 33, and 25 were identified as kaempferol-6-<em>C</em>-hexoside, kaempferol-8-<em>C</em>-hexoside, and dihydrokaempferol-6-<em>C</em>-hexoside, respectively.<sup><a href="#cit4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit4">4</a></sup></p>
<p>Two isomers of kaempferol were identified in both leaves and fruits extract, both yielding the same [M − H]<sup>−</sup> ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 298.96. The fragmentation pattern observed was typical of methoxylated flavonoids, with a key fragment at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 284.00 corresponding to [M-H-CH<sub>3</sub>]<sup>−</sup>. This fragmentation profile is consistent with that of isokaempferide (35) and rhamnocitrin (40).<sup><a href="#cit34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit34">34</a></sup></p>
<p>
<strong>
<em>Quercetin-based derivatives</em>
</strong>: A total of 11 quercetin derivatives were detected in the butanol extracts and tentatively identified as follows: peak 26 (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 5.55) presented a quasi-molecular ion peak at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 608.95 and a fragmentation pattern corresponding to a disaccharide residue consisting of rhamnose and hexose; the fragment ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 300.12[M-H-308(rutinose)]˙ is more intense than the ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 301.14[M-H-308(rutinose)]<sup>−</sup>, suggesting that a disaccharide is attached at the 3-OH position (ESI, Fig. S90). Quercetin-di-<em>O</em>-glycosides (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 8.39) appeared as a base peak at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 623.05, accompanied by daughter ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 477.10 and 447.08. The ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 477.10, resulting from the loss of a rhamnoside unit at first [M-H-146(rham)]<sup>−</sup>, suggests substitution at position 7. This is further supported by the identification of quercetin 7-<em>O</em>-rhamnoside (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 447.10) and previous studies indicating that the initial fragmentation of flavonol compounds typically involves cleavage of the <em>O</em>-glycosidic bond at position 7, followed by the breakdown of the moiety linked to position 3.<sup><a href="#cit35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit35">35</a></sup> Additionally, the appearance of the ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 447.08 [M-H-176(gluc)]<sup>−</sup> confirms the presence of a hexuornide unit at position 3. Based on this evidence, compound 37 is tentatively identified as quercetin 7-<em>O</em>-rhamnoside-3-<em>O</em>-hexuornide, found in both leaves and fruits.</p>
<p>Four isomers of dimethylquercetin glycosides were tentatively identified, presenting [M − H]<sup>−</sup> ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> (463.10, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 6.60 min)/(490.95, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 7.34 min)/(653.05, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 6.75 min)/(622.95, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 6.09 min) in the mass spectrum. The MS<sup>2</sup> spectra revealed fragment ions characteristic of <em>O</em>-glycosides. Among these, the ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 331.12[M-H-132]<sup>−</sup>, and 301.06[M-H-132-2CH<sub>3</sub>]<sup>−</sup>, correspond to dimethylquercetin-7-<em>O</em>-pentoside (31) which was specifically detected in leaves. The ions recognized in the leaves, stems, and fruits at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 329.05 [M-H-162(hex)], 314.13 [M-H-162(hex)-CH<sub>3</sub>]<sup>−</sup>, 299.03 [M-H-162(hex)-2CH<sub>3</sub>]<sup>−</sup>, prove the presence of dimethylquercetin-<em>O</em>-hexoside (36). Additionally, the appearance of daughter ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 491.09 [M-H-162(hex)]<sup>−</sup> and 329.17 [M-H-324(dihex)]<sup>−</sup> suggest the presence of two hexose units attached to the same hydroxyl group (OH) on the aglycone. Based on the evidence presented, metabolite 34 has been identified as dimethylquercetin di-<em>O</em>-hexoside.<sup><a href="#cit36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit36">36</a></sup></p>
<p>The detection of fragments associated with both <em>O</em>- and <em>C</em>-glycosylation patterns in leaves sample at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 491.11 [M-H-132]<sup>−</sup>, 341.08 [M-H-132-2CH<sub>3</sub>-120]<sup>−</sup>, confirm the presence of a dimethylquercetin di-glycoside structure (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> = 653.05, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 6.75) comprising pentose and hexose units. The sequential loss of a pentose unit followed by the cleavage of a hexose unit indicates that the pentoside occupies a terminal position, while the hexoside is directly linked to the aglycone, forming a pentosyl–hexoside arrangement. Additionally, the ion resulting from a 120 amu loss after the elimination of both the pentose and dimethyl groups supports the presence of <em>O</em>-pentosyl-<em>C</em>-hexoside moieties. The absence of fragment ions corresponding to the loss of 90 amu rules out attachment of the <em>C</em>-hexoside at the 6-<em>C</em> position and confirms its linkage at the 8-<em>C</em> position of the aglycone. Based on this evidence, the metabolite 28 has been identified as dimethylquercetin 7-<em>O</em>-pentoside-8-<em>C</em>-hexoside.</p>
<p>The analysis identified two mono <em>C</em>-glycosidic derivatives of quercetin in both leaves and stems extract. Metabolite 32 exhibited a base peak at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 463.10, with prominent MS fragments at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 372.87 [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup> and 342.97 [M-H-120]<sup>−</sup>. Notably, the absence of a fragment at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 355, which corresponds to the loss of a water molecule (18 amu) in addition to 90 amu [M-H-90-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup>, was critical in the structural elucidation. According to established guidelines for identifying isomeric 8-<em>C</em>-glycoside flavonoids, this compound was conclusively identified as quercetin-8-<em>C</em>-hexoside. Compound 29 (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 477.06, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 6.31) displayed a similar fragmentation pattern consistent with 8-<em>C</em>-hexosides, producing fragments at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 386.93 [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup>, 356.94 [M-H-120]<sup>−</sup>and 329.01 [Ag + 14]. Based on this fragmentation behavior, compound 29 was assigned as isorhamnetin-8-<em>C</em>-hexoside.<sup><a href="#cit37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit37">37</a></sup>.</p>
<p>Peak 30, found only in leaves extract, presented a molecular ion peak at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 623.3. In MS<sup>2</sup>, it suffered neutral losses of 132 amu (pentoside) and 176 amu (hexuornide), resulting in the aglycone ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 315.11. The aglycone was assigned as isorhamnetin, based on the loss of [M-H-132-176]<sup>−</sup> from the base peak at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 623.3. The discovery of a daughter ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 491.06 and a fragment at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 315.11 provided further structural insight. These fragments suggest that the pentoside occupies the terminal position in the diglycoside moiety, while the hexuornide is directly attached to the aglycone forming a pentosyl–hexuornide arrangement. Based on this information, the compound was proposed to be isorhamnetin-<em>O</em>-pentosyl hexuornide.</p>
<p>
<strong>
<em>Myrecetin-based derivatives</em>
</strong>: Two syringetin analogs labeled as peaks 27 and 41, were tentatively assigned for the first in <em>P. aculeata</em> using total ion chromatography (TIC) and base peak chromatography (BPC). These metabolites exhibited distinct elution times of 5.96 min and 10.53 min with precursor ion mass-to-charge ratios (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em>) of 683.01 and 639.08, respectively. Metabolite 27 was observed exclusively in the stem and presented a pseudomolecular ion peak at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 683.01. Its fragmentation pattern revealed daughter ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 521.04 [M-H-162(hex)]<sup>−</sup> and 345.23 [M-H-162(hex)-176(gluc)]<sup>−</sup>. These results indicate that the hexoside is located at the end of the diglycoside moiety, whereas the hexuornide is directly connected to the aglycone. This structural configuration corresponds to a hexosyl-hexuornide arrangement, leading to the tentative identification of this compound as syringetin <em>O</em>-hexosyl hexuornic acid. Metabolite 41 was detected across all examined plant parts and exhibited a pseudomolecular ion peak at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 639.08. The fragmentation pattern generated daughter ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 477.14 [M-H-162(hex)]<sup>−</sup> and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 345.15 [M-H-162(hex)-132(pent)]<sup>−</sup>, indicating a direct relationship between the hexosyl–pentoside disaccharide structure and the aglycone. Based on this evidence, compound 41 was identified as syringetin-<em>O</em>-hexosyl pentoside.</p></section><section id="sec26"><h6 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.2.2.2. Flavones</h6>
<p>
<strong>
<em>Apigenin-based derivatives</em>
</strong>: Five apigenin derivatives (compounds 44, 50, 51, 53 and 54) were identified in the butanol extract for the first time and distributed in all examined parts of <em>P. aculeate</em>. They confirmed by their fragmentation pattern and GNPS library (<a href="#fig3" class="usa-link">Fig. 3</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig3"><h6 class="obj_head">Fig. 3. Comprehensive molecular networking generated from MS/MS data in negative ionization mode for the butanol extracts of <em>P. aculeata</em> leaves, stems, and fruits. The nodes are annotated with parent masses and visualized as pie charts, where the colors yellow, purple, and green represent the relative distribution of precursor ion intensity across the respective plant parts. Clusters A–E contains nodes that correspond to matches in the GNPS spectral libraries.</h6>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12107345_d5ra00906e-f3.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/85d0/12107345/4296e0771274/d5ra00906e-f3.jpg" loading="lazy" height="418" width="738" alt="Fig. 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></figure><p>Mono-<em>C</em>-glycosylated derivatives of apigenin observed in cluster B exhibited key fragment ions at [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup> and [M-H-120]<sup>−</sup>, indicating the presence of 6-<em>C</em>- or 8-<em>C</em>-linked hexosides. The prominent ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 311.07 [M-H-120]<sup>−</sup> alongside a weaker fragment at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 341.04 [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup> suggests that the hexose is attached at the 8-<em>C</em> position. Consequently, compound 51 was tentatively identified as apigenin-8-<em>C</em>-hexoside. In contrast, the strong ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 341.03 [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup> with high intensity, followed by an abundant ion resulting from the loss of [M-H-90-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup>, indicates that the hexose is linked at the 6-<em>C</em> position. Thus, compound 54 was proposed to be apigenin-6-<em>C</em>-hexoside.</p>
<p>For di-<em>C</em>-glycosides observed in cluster B, the characteristic neutral losses produce fragment ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 503.11 [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup>, 473.10 [M-H-120]<sup>−</sup>, and 413.20 ([M-H-180]<sup>−</sup>), corresponding to sequential losses of hexose units. Additional fragmentation generates an ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 383.20 [M-H-90-120]<sup>−</sup>, which is compatible with [Ag + 113] and indicative of di-<em>C</em>-glycosyl flavone fragmentation. This pattern supports the existence of apigenin as the aglycone (MW = 270). Based on this fragmentation behavior and GNPS library (<a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a>), compound 44 was identified as an isomer of apigenin 6, 8-di-<em>C</em>-hexoside (vicenin-2).</p>
<p>Furthermore, the additional loss of an <em>O</em>-rhamnoside moiety, producing fragments at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 323.20 [M-H-90-146(rham)-18(H<sub>2</sub>O)]<sup>−</sup> and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 293.02 [M-H-120-146(rham)-H<sub>2</sub>O(18)]<sup>−</sup>, confirmed the triglycoside structure of apigenin and identified metabolite 53 as isovitexin <em>O</em>-rhamnoside.<sup><a href="#cit38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit38">38</a></sup> The apigenin aglycone (81) was tentatively identified in leaves and stems based on the precursor ion observed at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 268.96.</p>
<p>A minor peak was detected exclusively in the stem at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 445.03, with a retention time (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub>) of 4.512 min. Fragmentation analysis presented an ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 283.06 [M-H-162(hex)]<sup>−</sup>, characteristic of the acacetin aglycone (89), indicating the loss of a mono-hexose unit. This evidence led to the identification of the compound 50 as acacetin-<em>O</em>-hexoside.</p>
<p>
<strong>
<em>Luteolin-based derivatives</em>
</strong>: The total ion current (TIC) and base peak chromatograms (BPC) presented 8 luteolin mono-and di-hexosides; peaks 61, 66, 42, 46, 49, 52, 49, and 59 with molecular ions matching the following <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> values and retention times (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub>): 446.90 (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 5.818), 446.91 (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 8.407), 608.95 (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 1.529), 578.93 (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 3.01), 592.99 (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 3.809), 739.00 (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 5.428), 607.00 (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 3.767), and 593.06 (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 6.603), respectively. The tentative identification of these metabolites can be confirmed as follows.</p>
<p>A pair of mono-<em>C</em>-hexoside flavonoids with identical deprotonated precursor ions [M − H]<sup>−</sup> at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 446.90 were detected, expressing different retention times (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub>): 5.518 and 8.407 min. The MS<sup>2</sup> fragmentation gave ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 357.06/357.04, 327.11/327.06, and 298.96/299.10, resembling the fragmentation pattern characteristic of luteolin <em>C</em>-hexoside. The differentiation between 6-<em>C</em>- and 8-<em>C</em>-glycosidic linkages was determined based on diagnostic MS<sup>2</sup> and MS<sup>3</sup> fragment ions. Notably, the ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 428.95 [M-H-18(H<sub>2</sub>O)]<sup>−</sup> and 410.96 [M-H-36(2H<sub>2</sub>O)]<sup>−</sup>, along with the MS<sup>3</sup> ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 309, were characteristic of isoorientin(luteolin 6-<em>C</em>-hexoside) and were absent in orientin(luteolin 8-<em>C</em>-hexoside). The 6-<em>C</em>-glycoside was characterized by a prominent base peak at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 357.06, whereas the 8-<em>C</em>-glycoside displayed a base peak at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 327.06. Peak 61 presented ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 357.06 [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup>, 327.11 [M-H-90-18]<sup>−</sup>, 339 [M-H-120]<sup>−</sup>, [aglycone + 71]<sup>−</sup>, and [aglycone + 41]<sup>−</sup>. These represent a typical MS<sup>2</sup> fragmentation pattern of mono 6-<em>C</em>-glycosylated flavones.<sup><a href="#cit39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit39">39,40</a></sup> Based on the above findings, peaks 61 and 66 were detected in all analyzed plant parts and recognized as luteolin 6-<em>C</em>-hexoside and luteolin 8-<em>C</em>-hexoside, respectively. These compounds has been reported earlier in <em>Parkinsonia</em> species.<sup><a href="#cit11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit11">11</a></sup> Two analogs of di-<em>C</em>-glycosides of luteolin were detected in <em>P. aculeata</em> for the first time. They gave deprotonated precursor ions [M − H]<sup>−</sup> at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 608.95 and 578.93. The MS/MS spectral analysis of peak 42 presented typical fragmentation behavior for symmetrical di-<em>C</em>-glycosides. The observed daughter ions included [M-H-18(H<sub>2</sub>O)]<sup>−</sup>, [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup>, [M-H-120], [M-H-180]<sup>−</sup>, [M-H-210(90 + 120)]<sup>−</sup>, [M-H-240(120 + 120)]<sup>−</sup>, [Ag + 113]<sup>−</sup>, and [Ag+ 83]<sup>−</sup>, respectively. Key fragment ions were detected at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 399.10, 369.05, and 428.91 [M-H-180(90 + 90)]<sup>−</sup>. Based on these findings, compound 42 was preliminarily identified in both leaves and stems as luteolin 6, 8-di-<em>C</em>-hexoside. The typical fragmentation pattern of di-<em>C</em>-asymmetric glycosyl of luteolin (hexose and pentose) was observed at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 560.92 [M-H-18]<sup>−</sup>, 518.91 [M-H-60]<sup>−</sup>, 488.96 [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup>, 458.92 [M-H-120]<sup>−</sup>, and 428.97 [M-H-150]<sup>−</sup>. To identify the aglycone, the fragments at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 399.09 [Ag + 113] and 369.06 [Ag + 83] were examined, indicating a molecular weight of 285 amu for the aglycone. This mass likely corresponds to luteolin (88). The compound (46) was subsequently identified in both leaves and stems as luteolin-<em>C</em>-hexoside-<em>C</em>-pentoside.<sup><a href="#cit41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit41">41</a></sup></p>
<p>Two di-<em>O</em>-glycosylated flavones were found for the first time in stem. They produced different precursor ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> (607.00, 593.06) and identical daughter ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 285.00, indicating the neutral loss of their respective diglycoside moieties. For metabolite 48, a fragment loss of 322 amu was observed, which corresponds to the combined neutral loss of 146 amu from rhamnose (rham) and 176 amu from hexuornic acid (gluc). This confirmed the structure of metabolite 48 as luteolin-<em>O</em>-rhamnoside-<em>O</em>-hexuornide, representing its first reported occurrence in <em>P. aculeata</em>. Similarly, for metabolite 59, a neutral loss of 308 amu was detected, representing the combined loss of 162 amu from hexose (Hex) and 146 amu from rhamnose (rham). This established the identity of metabolite 59 as luteolin7-<em>O</em>-rutinoside, previously reported in the aqueous fraction of <em>P. aculeata.</em><sup><a href="#cit10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit10">10</a></sup></p>
<p>Compound 49, a highly abundant metabolite detected exclusively in stems, exhibited a precursor ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 592.99 and eluted at 3.909 min, along with five key fragment ions. The first fragment appeared at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 502.99 [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup>, while the second was observed at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 473.02 [M-H-120]<sup>−</sup>. Additional fragments presented characteristic patterns of a <em>C</em>-/<em>O</em>-dihexoside structure, including <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 357.18 [M-H-90-146(rham)]<sup>−</sup> and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 327.18 [M-H-120-146(rham)]<sup>−</sup>. These fragments correspond to (Ag + 71) and (Ag + 41), respectively, confirming the presence of <em>C</em>- and <em>O</em>-glycosidic moieties: a hexose linked <em>via</em> a <em>C</em>-glycosidic bond and a rhamnose linked <em>via</em> an <em>O</em>-glycosidic bond. Based on these data, metabolite 49 was identified as luteolin 8-<em>C</em>-hexoside <em>O</em>-rhamnoside. Peak 52 (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 5.428 min) exhibited a base peak observed exclusively in leaves at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 739.00. This corresponds to the molecular ion of peak 49 (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 593) with an additional 146 mass units, attributed to a rhamnosyl moiety. The MS fragmentation pattern strongly suggests the presence of luteolin triglycosides. Two key fragment ions appeared at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 327.42 [M-H-292(dirham)-120]<sup>−</sup> and 285.01[M-H-292(di-rham)-162(hex)]<sup>−</sup> proving compound 52 as luteolin 8-<em>C</em>-hexoside di-<em>O</em>-rhamnoside, representing its first reported occurrence in <em>P. aculeate</em>.</p>
<p>
<strong>
<em>Tricin-based derivatives</em>
</strong>: The butanol extracts analysis detected 6 tricin isomers, which were separated into two structural groups: methylated and non-methylated tricin glycosides. Mono-, di-, tri-, and tetra-<em>O</em>-glycosides were tentatively identified within both groups, based on characteristic mass losses associated with the fragmentation patterns of <em>O</em>-glycosides. The MS analysis of all isomers consistently generated fragment ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 329, suggesting the presence of a tricin aglycone (67), the loss of a glucuronic acid unit (176 amu) was a hallmark feature in MS/MS analysis of tricin-<em>O</em>-hexuornide (63), which was initially detected in both the stem and fruit and unreported in <em>P. aculeata</em>. Metabolite 65 presented a base ion signal at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> (593.00, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 8.207 min) and key ion fragments at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 447.07 [M-H-pentoside-CH<sub>3</sub>]<sup>−</sup>, and 329.22 [M-H-dipentoside]<sup>−</sup>, leading to its tentative identification as tricin-di-<em>O</em>-pentoside.<sup><a href="#cit10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit10">10</a></sup> A tri-glycoside compound (70) was tentatively assigned in leaves, stems, and fruits, and not recognized before in <em>P. aculeate.</em> It was proposed as tricin-<em>O</em>-rutinoside-<em>O</em>-pentoside. This proposal was based on prominent daughter ions resulting from the loss of rutinosyl pentoside structure (tri-saccharide) with key fragments observed at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 623.15 [M-H-146(rham)]<sup>−</sup>, 475.08 [M-H-162(hex)-132(pent)]<sup>−</sup>, 329.026 [M-H-146(rham)-162(hex)-132(pent)]<sup>−</sup>. The <em>tet-O</em>-glycoside tricin (71) gave base peak at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 931.06, similar to compound 70 but with the addition of a hexose unit. Key ions included <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 785.18 [M-H-146(rham)]<sup>−</sup>, 769.38 [M-H-162(hex)]<sup>−</sup>, 637.06 [M-H-162(hex)-132(pent)]<sup>−</sup>, 619.15 [M-H-162(hex)-132(pent)-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup>, and 461.21[M-H-146(rham)-324(dihex)]<sup>−</sup>. Based on these observations, metabolite 71 was detected in leaves and stems as tricin-<em>O</em>-rutinoside-<em>O</em>-hexoside-<em>O</em>-pentoside, not mentioned before in <em>P. aculeate</em>.</p>
<p>Peak 68 (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 639.00, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 10.565 min), discovered for the first time in <em>P. aculeate</em>. In our research it detected in both leaves and stems samples, and tentatively identified as a methylated tricin <em>O</em>-glycoside. Fragmentation analysis revealed the loss of a hexose moiety, producing a daughter ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 477.10 [M-H-162(hexose)]<sup>−</sup>. Further fragmentation generated an ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 329.17 [M-H-162(hexose)-132(pentose)-CH<sub>3</sub>]<sup>−</sup>, indicating the sequential loss of a pentose and a methyl group. These results strongly suggest the presence of a methylated tricin di-<em>O</em>-glycoside. The absence of a daughter ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 507 confirmed that both glycosides are attached at the same position on the aglycone, forming a diglycoside structure. Additionally, the presence of a daughter ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 447 (loss of hexose), followed by <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 329 (loss of pentose), indicates that the hexose moiety is in a terminal position, while the pentose is directly linked to the aglycone. This arrangement corresponds to a hexosyl–pentoside linkage.<sup><a href="#cit42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit42">42</a></sup> Based on these findings, compound 68 was tentatively identified as methyl-tricin <em>O</em>-hexosyl pentoside. Compound 69 (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 785.09, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 9.702 min) is structurally similar to compound 70, distinguished by the addition of a methyl group. Key ions detected include <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 639.24 [M-H-146(rha)]<sup>−</sup>, 491.16 [M-H-132(pent)-162(hex)]<sup>−</sup>, 473.12 [M-H-132(pent)-162(hex)-H<sub>2</sub>O]<sup>−</sup>, and 329.28 [M-H-146(rha)-162(hex)-132(pent)-CH<sub>3</sub>]<sup>−</sup>. These findings indicate that metabolite 69 is present in the leaves and stems as methyl-tricin <em>O</em>-rhamnoside-<em>O</em>-hexoside-<em>O</em>-pentoside, and was initially detected in <em>P. aculeate</em>.</p>
<p>
<strong>
<em>Diosmetin-based derivatives</em>
</strong>: Two diosmetin mono-<em>C</em>-hexosides with identical deprotonated precursor ions [M–H]<sup>−</sup> at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 460.99 were identified, exhibiting distinct time elution (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub>) of 6.297 and 6.417 min. MS<sup>2</sup> fragmentation presented ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 371.01 [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup>; [Ag + 41] and 341.04 [M-H-120]<sup>−</sup>; [Ag + 71], consistent with the characteristic fragmentation patterns of diosmetin 6-<em>C</em>- and 8-<em>C</em>-hexoside. Differentiation between the two compounds was achieved through diagnostic MS<sup>2</sup> and MS<sup>3</sup> fragment ions. Specifically, the ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 353.08 [M-H-90-18(H<sub>2</sub>O)]<sup>−</sup>, accompanied by MS<sup>3</sup> ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 309, was a unique marker for diosmetin-6-<em>C</em>-hexoside (56) and was absent in diosmetin-8-<em>C</em>-hexoside (58). Diosmetin-6-<em>C</em>-hexoside was exclusively identified in leaves, whereas diosmetin-8-<em>C</em>-hexoside was found across all examined plant parts.<sup><a href="#cit43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit43">43</a></sup> Similarly, peak 45, characterized by the additional loss of a <em>C</em>-pentoside [M-H-60]<sup>−</sup> at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 533.04 and [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup> at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 503.00, alongside key ions characteristic of di-<em>C</em>-glycosides at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 383 [Ag + 83] and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 413.01 [Ag + 113]. The observed molecular weight of 299 amu corresponds to the aglycone diosmetin (90). The fragmentation pattern includes a daughter ion resulting from the loss of a water molecule at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 575.03 [M-H-18(H<sub>2</sub>O)]<sup>−</sup>, followed by ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 533.04 [M-H-60]<sup>−</sup> and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 503 [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup>. These characteristic fragments confirm the presence of a pentoside attached at the 6-<em>C</em> position of the aglycone and a hexoside at the 8-<em>C</em> position. Based on this evidence, compound 45 was identified in leaves, stems, and fruits as diosmetin 8-<em>C</em>-hexoside 6-<em>C</em>-pentoside.<sup><a href="#cit44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit44">44</a></sup></p>
<p>The detection of a daughter ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 298.96 [M-H-162(hex)]<sup>−</sup> indicates that peak 62 corresponds to diosmetin-<em>O</em>-hexoside. Additionally, the presence of a daughter ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 475.05, representing the loss of a pentose, followed by an ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 298.99, signifying the loss of a hexuornide, provides insights into the molecule's structural arrangement. Specifically, the pentose moiety occupies the terminal position, while the hexuornide group is directly attached to the aglycone, consistent with a pentosyl–hexuornide linkage. Based on this evidence, metabolite 60 was tentatively identified as diosmetin <em>O</em>-pentosyl hexuornide, detected for the first time exclusively in the fruit extract.</p>
<p>Two isomers of chrysoeriol were identified in <em>P. aculeata</em> and tentatively assigned based on their precursor ions and retention times (<em>R</em><sub>t</sub>). The first isomer, a symmetrical di-<em>C</em>-glycoside, was detected at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 623.01 with a retention time of 2.291 min, while the second isomer, an asymmetrical <em>C</em>-/<em>O</em>-glycoside, appeared at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 606.98 with a retention time of 5.670 min. The symmetrical di-<em>C</em>-glycoside isomer exhibited characteristic fragment ions that correspond to the stepwise loss of two hexose residues. These included <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 533.06 [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup>, <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 503.05 [M-H-120]<sup>−</sup>, and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 383.04 [M-H-240]<sup>−</sup>, along with fragments at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 413.05 [M-H-120-90]<sup>−</sup>, corresponding to [Ag + 83], and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 533.06, corresponding to [Ag + 113]. Based on this fragmentation pattern, compound 43 was tentatively identified in the leaves and stems as chrysoeriol 6,8-di-<em>C</em>-hexoside. The asymmetrical <em>C</em>-/<em>O</em>-glycosides were identified as the predominant isomers in leaves, producing daughter ions characteristic of <em>C</em>- and <em>O</em>-linked hexoses. These ions were detected at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 486.96 [M-H-120]<sup>−</sup>, 341.16 [M-H-120-146(rham)]<sup>−</sup>, 323.18 [M-H-120-146(rham)-18(H<sub>2</sub>O)]<sup>−</sup>, and 308 [M-H-120-146(rham)-18(H<sub>2</sub>O)-CH<sub>3</sub>]<sup>−</sup>. The fragmentation pattern corresponds to the sequential loss of a <em>C</em>-hexoside and an <em>O</em>-rhamnoside. The lack of a fragment characteristic of 6-<em>C</em>-hexosides at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 517 [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup> confirms that the <em>C</em>-hexosides are linked to the aglycone at the 8-<em>C</em> position. Based on these findings, metabolite 55 was identified as chrysoeriol 8-<em>C</em>-hexoside <em>O</em>-rhamnoside.<sup><a href="#cit45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit45">45</a></sup></p>
<p>We identified mono- and di-<em>O</em>-pinocembrin hexosides for the first time in leaves and fruits of <em>P. aculeata</em>. These compounds were tentatively characterized based on their precursor ions, detected at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> (416.98, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 7.953) and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> (579.01, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 6.365), respectively. The key diagnostic fragmentation pattern for pinocembrin derivatives was observed in both compounds 64 and 57, marked by a prominent ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 255.05/254.98 characteristic for pinocembrin aglycone. In the MS analysis of compound 64, the loss of a hexose unit produced a characteristic daughter ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 255.05 [M-H-162(gl)]<sup>−</sup>, confirming its identity as pinocembrin-<em>O</em>-hexoside. Similarly, the MS analysis of compound 57 revealed the sequential loss of hexose moieties, evidenced by diagnostic daughter ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 417.09 [M-H-162(hex)]<sup>−</sup> and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 254.98 [M-H-324(dihex)]<sup>−</sup>, confirming its characterization as pinocembrin-di-<em>O</em>-hexoside. The pinocembrin aglycone (85) was assigned in all examined parts at <em>m</em>/z (255.12, <em>R</em><sub>t</sub> = 8.256).</p></section><section id="sec27"><h6 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.2.2.3. Detection of non-colored flavonoids (flavan-3-ols)</h6>
<p>Catechin (74), and (+)-gallocatechin (75) were viewed as a cluster comprising two linked nodes (C) (<a href="#fig3" class="usa-link">Fig. 3</a>). The results presented in <a href="#tab1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> highlight the relative abundance of base ions detected for the first time exclusively in fruit at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 451.12, 289.00, and 305.00 in negative ion mode. These ions serve as key markers for the identification of catechin hexosides (73), catechin (74), and (+)-gallocatechin (75), respectively.</p></section><section id="sec28"><h6 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.2.2.4. Flavanone-based derivatives</h6>
<p>The fragmentation behavior of naringenin derivatives in a butanol extract is defined by the neutral loss of pseudo-molecular ions [M − H]<sup>−</sup>, producing a dominant fragment ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 271, corresponding to the naringenin aglycone. Additional fragment ions include <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 299.96 [M-H-OCH<sub>3</sub>]<sup>−</sup>, indicative of methoxylated naringenin (76) in the stem, and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 417.05 [M-H-162(hex)]<sup>−</sup>. The ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 271.14 [M-H-162(hex)-146(rham)]<sup>−</sup> represents naringenin-<em>O</em>-hexose-<em>O</em>-rhamnoside (78) in the leaves, while <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 447.06 [M-H-146(rham)]<sup>−</sup> and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 417.04 [M-H-176(hex)]<sup>−</sup> are characteristic of naringenin-<em>O</em>-rhamnoside-<em>O</em>-hexuornide (79) in leaves. Fragments at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 343.03 [M-H-90]<sup>−</sup> and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 313.07 [M-H-120]<sup>−</sup> are associated with naringenin-8-<em>C</em>-hexoside (77) found in both leaves and stems. These fragmentation patterns highlight the structural diversity of naringenin derivatives and their specific glycosylation and methylation modifications.<sup><a href="#cit46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit46">46</a></sup></p></section><section id="sec29"><h6 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.2.2.5. Dihydrochalcones</h6>
<p>The total ion current (TIC) and base peak chromatogram (BPC) analyses identified three dihydrochalcone compounds (91–93) with molecular ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 272.99, 434.89, and 435.01, along with distinctive fragment ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 389.17, 315.13 [M-H-120]<sup>−</sup>, and 273.03 [M-H-162(hex)]<sup>−</sup>, respectively.<sup><a href="#cit47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit47">47</a></sup> These compounds, previously unreported in <em>P. aculeata</em>, were confirmed as phloretin-<em>C</em>-hexoside, phloretin-<em>O</em>-hexoside (phlorizin or nothofagin), and their aglycone at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 272.99.</p></section></section></section></section></section><section id="sec30"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Antibacterial activity</h2>
<p>The disc diffusion test was conducted on seven microorganisms, including Gram-positive strains (<em>S. aureus</em> ATCC 29213 and <em>S. aureus</em>) and Gram-negative strains (<em>E. coli</em> ATCC 25922, <em>E. coli</em>, <em>P. aeruginosa</em> ATCC 27853, <em>P. aeruginosa</em>, and <em>K. pneumonia</em>). The control treatment (60% ethanol) showed no inhibitory activity against any of the tested microorganisms. Vancomycin (30 μg) was utilized as a positive control to assess the antibacterial efficacy of the plant extracts acting as a reference standard; it exhibited significant inhibition zones across all tested bacterial strains. The outcomes of these tests are summarized in <a href="#tab3" class="usa-link">Tables 3–5</a>.</p>
