<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/tab5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="tab5fna">
<sup>a</sup><p class="display-inline">The 60% ethanol control treatment exhibited no inhibitory effects on any of the tested microorganisms.</p>
</div></div></section><p>In the disc diffusion technique, all tested extracts exhibited significant activity against all strains at a high concentration of 2 mg mL<sup>−1</sup> in comparison with the result of vancomycin (30 μg) as a positive control. The butanol extract from leaves demonstrated the strongest activity, with inhibition zones of 20.13 mm against <em>S. aureus</em> ATCC 29213, 14.42 mm against <em>E. coli</em>, and 14.35 mm against <em>E. coli</em> ATCC 25922. For the remaining strains, the inhibition zones ranged from 11 mm to 14 mm at the same concentration. The butanol extract from stems exhibited the highest activity against <em>E. coli</em> ATCC 25922 (13.17 mm), <em>P. aeruginosa</em> ATCC 27853 (13.02 mm), and <em>E. coli</em> (13.01 mm). For the other strains, the inhibition zones ranged from 9 mm to 12 mm at a concentration of 2 mg mL<sup>−1</sup>. The butanol extract from fruits displayed the highest activity against <em>K. pneumonia</em> (13.41 mm), <em>E. coli</em> ATCC 25922 (13.06 mm), and <em>P. aeruginosa</em> ATCC 27853 (11.23 mm). For the remaining strains, the inhibition zones ranged from 10 mm to 11 mm at a concentration of 2 mg mL<sup>−1</sup>. Among all the tested extracts, leaf extract was the most effective against all microorganisms. However, the fruits butanol extract demonstrated the strongest antibacterial activity specifically against <em>K. pneumonia</em> compared to the other extracts.</p>
<p>A two-fold serial dilution approach was used to determine the minimum inhibitory concentrations (MICs) of the butanol extracts from the investigated plant organs. The values ranged from 250 to 0.47 mg mL<sup>−1</sup>. The experiments were run in duplicate against seven distinct bacterial strains, including Gram-positive and Gram-negative species. <a href="#tab5" class="usa-link">Table 5</a> shows the MIC values, represented as mean ± standard error (S.E.) (<a href="#tab6" class="usa-link">Table 6</a>).</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="tab6"><h3 class="obj_head">Table 6. MIC of butanol extracts from examined plant parts against seven microorganisms by microbroth dilution technique<a href="#tab6fna" class="usa-link"><sup>a</sup></a>.</h3>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/tab6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="tab6fna">
<sup>a</sup><p class="display-inline">Note: values are expressed in (mg mL<sup>−1</sup>).</p>
</div></div></section><p>The results of the disk diffusion assay showed moderate inhibition zones ranging from approximately 11 to 14 mm for most extracts, indicating notable antibacterial effects. These findings were further confirmed by the MIC values, which ranged between 4.0 and 8.5 mg mL<sup>−1</sup> for the majority of the tested strains. This close correlation between the two methods reflects the reliability of the antibacterial potential of the extracts. Specifically, leaves extract demonstrated the strongest antibacterial activity, with lower MIC values (4.0–6.0 mg mL<sup>−1</sup>) against both standard and clinical isolates of <em>E. coli</em> and <em>P. aeruginosa</em>, consistent with their relatively larger inhibition zones observed in the disk diffusion assay. Similarly, the MIC of 1.5 mg mL<sup>−1</sup> against <em>S. aureus</em> (ATCC29213) supports the large inhibition zone (20.13 ± 1.48 mm) observed, confirming the high sensitivity of this strain to the leaves extract. The stems extract also showed effective antibacterial activity with MIC values ranging from 4.8 to 8.5 mg mL<sup>−1</sup>. The inhibition zones recorded for the stems extract, although slightly smaller compared to the leaves extract, were still significant and aligned with the MIC data. Notably, the MIC against <em>K. pneumoniae</em> reached 8.5 mg mL<sup>−1</sup>, matching the smaller inhibition zone (9.40 ± 1.02 mm) obtained for this bacterium. Fruits extract exhibited comparable MIC values (4.6–7.1 mg mL<sup>−1</sup>), which corresponded well with the observed inhibition zones in the disk diffusion assay. In particular, <em>K. pneumoniae</em> showed a relatively low MIC value (4.6 ± 0.3 mg mL<sup>−1</sup>) against fruits extract, consistent with a moderate inhibition zone size. Overall, the data revealed that all parts of the plant possess notable antibacterial activities, with leaves extract generally showing the highest potency.<sup><a href="#cit13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit13">13,14</a></sup> The consistency between the disk diffusion and MIC results emphasizes the potential of the butanol extracts as promising antibacterial agents. The relatively low MIC values obtained, especially for <em>S. aureus</em>, <em>E. coli</em>, and <em>P. aeruginosa</em>, further highlight the efficacy of these extracts and suggest their possible application in the development of new antimicrobial agents.</p>
<p>The MIC and disk diffusion findings show that <em>P. aculeata</em> leaves and stems extracts have equivalent antibacterial activity, which is most likely owing to identical phytochemical composition. In contrast, the fruits extract had reduced effectiveness, indicating a different profile of bioactive components.</p>
<p>The butanol extracts showed strong antibacterial effects because they contain a lot of different chemicals, including important secondary metabolites found through LC-MS analysis. Several antibacterial compounds have been detected in <em>P. aculeata</em> extracts, as reported by Hosseini Doust 2018.<sup><a href="#cit48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit48">48</a></sup> The primary components of butanol extracts include <em>O</em>-caffeoylquinic acid, caffeic acid, gallic acid, dihydrokaempferol-6-<em>C</em>-hexoside, quercetin-3-<em>O</em>-rutinoside, dimethylquercetin-7-<em>O</em>-pentoside-8-<em>C</em>-hexoside, kaempferol, luteolin-8-<em>C</em>-hexoside-<em>O</em>-rhamnoside, luteolin-8-<em>C</em>-hexoside (orientin), naringenin-8-<em>C</em>-hexoside, apigenin, acacetin, and pinoresinol. The antibacterial effects of all these components are outlined below. Various studies have highlighted the inhibitory effects of plant flavonoid-rich extracts and pure flavonoids against certain pathogenic bacteria. It has also been noted that the presence of hydroxyl groups at different positions on the A and B rings of flavonoids enhance their antibacterial activity. Hydroxyl groups (OH) at positions 5, 3′, and 4′ on the A and B rings, along with a methoxy group (OMe) at C-5′, have demonstrated significant activity against <em>S. aureus</em>. In the A ring, several studies have confirmed that the combined hydroxylation at positions 5 and 7 plays a crucial role in the antibacterial activity of flavonols against <em>S. aureus</em> strains.<sup><a href="#cit49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit49">49</a></sup> Additionally, research by Bitchagno <em>et al.</em>, 2015 (ref. <a href="#cit50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit50">50</a>) revealed that tetraflavonoids lacking hydroxyl groups on the C ring exhibit moderate activity against <em>E. coli</em>. The <em>O</em>-caffeoylquinic acid isomers have demonstrated antibacterial activity against <em>S. aureus</em>, <em>E. coli</em>, <em>P. aeruginosa</em>, and <em>K. pneumonia</em>.<sup><a href="#cit51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit51">51</a></sup> The fruit's strong antimicrobial effect against <em>K. pneumonia</em> is attributed to the high abundance of <em>O</em>-caffeoylquinic acid isomers in its extract. Caffeic acid has been identified as having antibacterial properties when tested both individually and in combination with plant extracts, showing activity against clinical strains of <em>S. aureus</em>.<sup><a href="#cit52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit52">52,53</a></sup> Additionally, studies have revealed that gallic acid and its methyl ester exhibit antibacterial activity against <em>S. aureus</em> and <em>E. coli</em>.<sup><a href="#cit54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit54">54</a></sup> Flavonols, including kaempferol, quercetin, myricetin, and their derivatives, have demonstrated effectiveness against <em>S. aureus</em>, <em>E. coli</em>, and <em>K. pneumonia</em> strains.<sup><a href="#cit55" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit55">55</a></sup> Similarly, flavones such as luteolin, naringenin, apigenin, acacetin, diosmetin, and their isomers exhibit antibacterial properties against <em>S. aureus</em> and <em>E. coli</em>.<sup><a href="#cit49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="cit49">49</a></sup> Leaves extract stands out as having the strongest antibacterial activity, likely due to the high abundance of luteolin and kaempferol derivatives in its composition.</p></section><section id="sec31"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">5. Conclusion</h2>
<p>The butanol extracts derived from leaves, stems, and fruits of <em>P. aculeata</em> presented notable antibacterial activity against seven bacterial strains, including both Gram-positive (<em>Staphylococcus aureus</em> ATCC 29213 and <em>S. aureus</em>) and Gram-negative (<em>Escherichia coli</em> ATCC 25922, <em>E. coli</em>, <em>Pseudomonas aeruginosa</em> ATCC 27853, <em>P. aeruginosa</em>, and <em>Klebsiella pneumoniae</em>). This antimicrobial effect is likely due to the high concentration of secondary metabolites identified through LC-MS/MS analysis, which are believed to play a pivotal role in microbial inhibition. These findings highlight the potential of <em>P. aculeata</em> as a promising source for developing natural treatments for various infectious diseases. However, further studies are required to comprehensively evaluate the safety profile and potential side effects of these extracts prior to their medicinal application.</p></section><section id="sec32"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Data availability</h2>
<p>The authors affirm that the data underpinning the findings of this research are fully accessible within the article.</p>
<p>
<em>Molecular networking</em>: The raw data files generated from LC-ESI-MS/MS analysis were converted into mzXML format using the MS Convert tool from ProteoWizard (version 3.0.21050, <a href="https://proteowizard.org" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://proteowizard.org</a>). Once converted, the data were uploaded to the MassIVE Data sets repository <em>via</em> WinSCP (<a href="https://massive.ucsd.edu" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://massive.ucsd.edu</a>). The resulting molecular network was exported as a GraphML file and visualized using a force-directed layout in Cytoscape version 3.7.1.</p>
<p>
<em>HPLC-ESI-MS</em>/<em>MS</em>: The MS-DIAL software version 4.70 and the Fiehn HILIC library were used to identify the constituents.</p></section><section id="sec33"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Author contributions</h2>

Food Biochem. 2024;2024(1):9994944. doi: 10.1155/jfbc/9994944.</cite> [<a href="https://doi.org/10.1155/jfbc/9994944" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">DOI</a>] [<a href="https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?journal=J.%20Food%20Biochem.&amp;title=Flavonoids%20Isolated%20From%20Delphinium%20semibarbatum%20Flowering%20Aerial%20Parts%20With%20Their%20Antibacterial,%20Antibiofilm,%20and%20Antiswarming%20Activity%20Against%20Proteus%20mirabilis%20and%20Staphylococcus%20aureus&amp;author=M.%20Lotfaliani&amp;author=S.%20A.%20Ayatollahi&amp;author=F.%20Kobarfard&amp;author=M.%20B.%20Majnooni&amp;author=A.%20Mirzaei&amp;volume=2024&amp;issue=1&amp;publication_year=2024&amp;pages=9994944&amp;doi=10.1155/jfbc/9994944&amp;" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Google Scholar</a>]</li>
</ol></section></section><section id="_ad93_" lang="en" class="associated-data"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Associated Data</h2>
<p class="font-secondary"><em>This section collects any data citations, data availability statements, or supplementary materials included in this article.</em></p>
<section id="_adsm93_" lang="en" class="supplementary-materials"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplementary Materials</h3>
<section class="sm xbox font-sm" id="db_ds_supplementary-material1_reqid_"><div class="caption p"><span>RA-015-D5RA00906E-s001</span></div>
<div class="media p"><div class="caption">
<a href="/articles/instance/12107345/bin/RA-015-D5RA00906E-s001.pdf" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" class="usa-link">RA-015-D5RA00906E-s001.pdf</a><sup> (4.3MB, pdf) </sup>
</div></div></section></section><section id="_adda93_" lang="en" class="data-availability-statement"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Data Availability Statement</h3>
<p>The authors affirm that the data underpinning the findings of this research are fully accessible within the article.</p>
<p>
<em>Molecular networking</em>: The raw data files generated from LC-ESI-MS/MS analysis were converted into mzXML format using the MS Convert tool from ProteoWizard (version 3.0.21050, <a href="https://proteowizard.org" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://proteowizard.org</a>). Once converted, the data were uploaded to the MassIVE Data sets repository <em>via</em> WinSCP (<a href="https://massive.ucsd.edu" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://massive.ucsd.edu</a>). The resulting molecular network was exported as a GraphML file and visualized using a force-directed layout in Cytoscape version 3.7.1.</p>
<p>
<em>HPLC-ESI-MS</em>/<em>MS</em>: The MS-DIAL software version 4.70 and the Fiehn HILIC library were used to identify the constituents.</p></section></section></section><footer class="p courtesy-note font-secondary font-sm text-center"><hr class="headless">
<p>Articles from RSC Advances are provided here courtesy of <strong>Royal Society of Chemistry</strong></p></footer></section></article>
